的终点是最终响应。表中文字并未逐一规定空角色、心跳或其他非内容响应的过滤，因此不能只凭这个名字认定它等于本库“首个有效内容”的边界。接入工具时需要核验实际导出事件与计数映射。\n\n| 文档中的量 | 归约对象与分母 | 提炼时必须保留的区别 |\n|---|---|---|\n| TTFT | 每请求一个发出到首响应的时间 | 首响应、首有效内容、服务端首 token 是不同候选事件 |\n| Time to Second Token | 每请求第一与第二个 streaming response 的间隔 | 名称含 token，文字定义使用 response；多 token chunk 时不能推断逐 token 轨迹 |\n| ITL | 每个相邻响应间隔除以后一个响应中的生成 token 数 | 这是响应级归一化观测，不是已经观测到全部真实 token 间隔 |\n| 每用户输出速率 | 每请求不含第一个 token 的输出量除以该请求生成阶段历时 | 每请求速率的平均、合并间隔的倒数与系统总输出速率不同 |\n| Request latency | 每请求发出到最终响应 | 最终协议响应可以晚于最后一个内容 token |\n| 输入/输出序列长度 | 每请求 token 数 | 分词器、模板、特殊 token 和输出过滤共同影响计数 |\n| 输出 token 吞吐 | benchmark 中输出 token 总数除以 benchmark 历时 | 必须保留输出人口和实际计时窗，不能用各请求速率求和替代 |\n| 请求吞吐 | benchmark 中最终响应数除以 benchmark 历时 | 最终响应、质量合格完成与满足联合时延条件的完成并非同一个集合 |\n\nREADME 的测量区间说明按区间内完成的请求收集观测；这一完成窗口可以包含窗前到达者，并排除窗末尚未完成者。它与固定到达 cohort 加排空截止的设计不同。工具依据最近若干测量的变化执行的稳定性规则，也不证明生产队列已经稳定或尾部概率已被充分估计。\n\ngoodput 文档将其定义为每秒满足指定指标约束的已完成请求数，并以 TTFT、ITL 约束作例。这里的工具约束不包含一个自动成立的任务质量证明；必须另行确定质量评价、完整 offered 人口、失败与拒绝规则。特定例子的阈值不是本库默认 SLO。\n\nGPU telemetry 表提供功率、能量、显存、时钟和利用率等观测入口。指标可被采集不等于其分子、单位、采样率、设备范围和实验边界已经与服务计数对齐；它们也不能单独解释吞吐瓶颈。\n\n## InfraAgents 如何据此推导\n\n本库依据这些文档建立明确的指标合同：事件起止、时钟、单位、请求人口、输出计数、统计单位、窗口和未定义原因。逐 token 可见时间已知时，TPOT 使用首末内容时间与 N−1 个间隔；若改用协议最终响应，其多出的尾部必须单列。对照实验还需明确实际发送与计划到达的差、每用户速率与系统吞吐、成功条件与联合 SLO，以及离线质量判定和服务完成的两个截止。这些是本库在固定资料上的推导，不能归称为工具已经实现的全部审计功能。\n\n具体算法与反例见[服务指标](../workloads/metric-events.md)；负载、区组、估计对象和确认实验见[评测设计](../workloads/measurement-comparability.md)；联合资格和容量边界见[Goodput](../workloads/slo-goodput.md)。\n\n## "e210de2929034d4a6e25a31ee72b81a184412a7e637c979210f91ae02a261b04", "reading_scope": "full", "content": "# S-MLPERF-SCENARIOS：MLCommons — Inference Mobile Scenarios\n\n## 本地完整原文\n\n[文档原文](../../references/S-MLPERF-SCENARIOS/upstream/loadgen/README.md)\n\n固定版本：Git 3ecc2e0a0c126503debec8b8213b1be949f04636 (2026-04-22)。来源版本日期：2026-04-22；整理核对日期：2026-10-22。\n\n## 到达过程为什么改变问题\n\n归档的 LoadGen 场景注释区分闭环与独立到达。SingleStream 每次发一个样本，完成后再发下一个；MultiStream 一次查询包含多个样本，也在前一查询完成后继续，查询延迟由其中最晚样本完成决定。Server 按随机 Poisson 到达过程提供请求，用目标到达率和延迟条件评价；Offline 一次交付整个样本集合，关注完成吞吐。\n\n```pseudocode\n闭环：发送查询 → 等待其完成 → 生成下一查询\n独立到达：按给定到达过程产生计划时间 → 按计划发出 → 分别记录完成\n离线：给出整个输入集合 → 执行 → 按完整完成量与完成时间计吞吐\n```\n\n闭环会在系统变慢时自动减少后续压力。由此得到的平均吞吐或延迟，不能直接代表同一名义速率下的独立到达服务。多样本查询的最大完成时间也不等于样本延迟的平均值。\n\n## 范围与可用推导\n\n这些定义来自固定 LoadGen 实现的注释，不替代正式评测规则；原文也说明冲突时规范优先。这里不迁移某项基准的阈值。比较服务时需固定到达模型、观察人口、拒绝/失败和计时边界，再评价[到达与并发](../workloads/arrival-and-concurrency.md)及[Goodput](../workloads/slo-goodput.md)。\n"}, {"path": 本地完整原文\n\n[文档原文](../../references/S-TORCH-PROFILER/upstream/docs/source/profiler.md)\n\n固定版本：Git 647b6a2ff9a83a886464932dfad78781ceb61cb6 (2026-04-30)。来源版本日期：2026-04-30；整理核对日期：2026-10-22。\n\n## 从操作归属到时间窗口\n\n框架 profiler 的价值是把高层操作、设备工作、输入形状、调用栈与内存活动放到可联系的记录中。归档支持等待、预热、记录与记录后保存等状态；调用方推进步计数，采样窗口按状态切换。预热初始化采集路径，实际输出只覆盖所选记录窗口，不能把未采集区间解释成没有工作。\n\n```pseudocode\n定义要区分的问题与所需事件，选择采集活动和字段\n按步推进等待、预热和记录状态\n在记录窗口收集操作/设备/内存信息，窗口结束时保存\n依据操作与设备事件的关联解释瓶颈，再用普通执行检验端到端结果\n```\n\n形状与栈采集增加开销，部分形状跟踪会保留张量引用，可能改变引用计数相关优化和内存表现。设备事件区间与 host 调用区间也不是同一计时边界。详细采集结果适合诊断，服务性能结论需要说明采集扰动并由相同条件下的普通执行支持。\n\n## 应怎样检验解释\n\n保持输入与设备条件相同，改变是否采集形状/栈和采集窗口，观察时间与峰值内存是否随之改变。若某优化只在 profiler 下变快，先检查采集路径与生命周期变化。机制展开见[框架 profiler](../tools/framework-profiler-scope.md)；混合归档中的文档原文保留可读，源码仅作核验。\n"}, {"path": "knowledge/sources/S-NCU.md", "sha256": "1bec5edf1e9343ad79094b2772466c3132597f8f83085d1d8959683c187f9f17", prefill 与 decode 的资源分配和并行选择解耦，并把阶段间状态通信与链路条件纳入服务目标。\n\n## InfraAgents 如何据此推导\n\n本库将总路径拆成两侧排队、计算、KV 转换与传输，说明分离收益必须超过新增通信和接管成本。\n\n## 适用边界\n\n所列机制与接口资料；本文设计推导和数值例为独立分析，不是目标机器实测。 原始版本、日期依据与归档范围见本地 "range_unit": "one-based inclusive lines", "ranges": [[696, 870], [1381, 1690], [2033, 2160], [2307, 2407]], "content": "LINES 696-870\ntion, although they did not make it to introductory textbooks (more information and\nreferences can be found in [Beyene and Moineddin 2005; Schaarschmidt 2007; Dilba\net al. 2007]). From several available methods, we have a closer look at two. One is\nbased on statistical simulation (bootstrap) [Davison and Hinkley 1997]. The other is\nbased on Fieller’s theorem [Fieller 1954], which gives a conﬁdence interval for the ratio\nof means of normally distributed variables. We show how both methods can be applied\nin the experimental design we propose, and empirically evaluate them.\n4.3. Related Efforts\nSome of the things we propose here have been proposed earlier in computer science,\nbut have not been widely adopted.\nA bootstrap-based method for construction of conﬁdence intervals is being used in\nthe Haskell community, supported by the Criterion benchmarking library [O’Sullivan\n2009]. The library is for measuring performance of one system only in single-level\nexperiments. Apart from using robust methods it has also the advantage of detecting\noutliers and auto-corellation of the data. The tool is based on [Boyer 2008].\nConﬁdence interval for speed-up has been proposed by Luo and John [2004] in\nthe context of processor simulation. The uncertainty does not come from random ef-\nfects, but rather from random sub-sampling – only randomly chosen execution inter-\nvals from the whole application execution are simulated, the same intervals in both\nof the systems to compare. The conﬁdence interval proposed for this problem comes\nfrom [Cochran 1977], it is a parametric interval based on asymptotic normality of not\nonly the means, but also their ratio, which is only possible for large samples. The\nmethod by Fieller [1954] that we use in this work is more general.\n5. OUTLINE OF THE NEW METHOD\nThe rest of our paper includes a detailed description of our quantiﬁcation method and\nits empirical evaluation based on real benchmark results. We offer two alternative\ndescriptions of our method. Section 6 provides a guide for practitioners, while Sec-\ntion 7 is a variant for scientists with some statistical background. Section 6 describes\nthe method in practical terms and with recommendations on how it can be used. Sec-\ntion 7 formulates the method in statistical terms, gives the assumptions and necessary\nproofs, and discusses further alternatives. We use the terminology common in the ﬁeld.\nIn statistical terms, the core of the method is our statistical model of random effects\nin n-way classiﬁcation, which models performance in one system. We apply it indepen-\ndently to both systems we aim to compare. For clarity, the model is described for 2-way\nclassiﬁcation ﬁrst (3 levels in the experiment, Section 7.1.1), but later in the general\nform of n-way classiﬁcation (an arbitrary number of levels, Section 7.1.2). While we\nkeep referring to ‘execution time’ in the statistics to make presentation concrete, the\nmodel is sufﬁciently general for any response variable, given the stated assumptions.\nWe then show how to construct the conﬁdence interval for the mean within this model,\nstill for the mean of each system separately (Section 7.2). We give two alternative\nmethods, a parametric method based on asymptotic normality and a non-parametric\nmethod based on bootstrap. Later we show how to construct the conﬁdence interval for\nthe ratio of the means of two systems, again using a parametric and non-parametric\nmethod (Section 7.3). The non-parametric bootstrap method is a natural extension of\nthe method for a single system. The parametric method uses Fieller’s theorem [Fieller\n1954].\n\n\n=== PDF page 14 ===\n14\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\n6. FOR PRACTITIONERS: THE NEW METHOD WITHOUT STATISTICS\nThe technique we propose here comprises of the actual statistical method (proven and\nprecisely deﬁned in the following section) and of practical advice how to use it. We feel\nthat it is important to stress that while the statistical method is general, our advice\nmay not be. The practicalities depend on the context (the goal of the study, the critical-\nity of the results and the consequences of possible errors, the kind of computer system\nevaluated, the runtime environment, infrastructure, and benchmarks). We understand\nthat having a single best technique that could be mechanically followed in every study\nmight be appealing, but we strongly believe that such practice is an illusion, and we\ndeﬁnitely do not provide one here.\n6.1. Designing the Experiments\nWe describe our technique sequentially, but in practice it would be an iterative process.\nAt the end of this section, we provide a small worked example.\nInitial number of levels. When planning experiments, we ﬁrst decide what the high-\nest level of the experiment should be. All factors inﬂuencing performance above that\nlevel need to be controlled (and documented). The highest level might be compilation\n(of the benchmark, runtime environment, or all). If compilation is found to be fully\ndeterministic, this may not be needed. Though note that even symbol names have im-\npact on performance [Gu et al. 2004], so we may choose to randomize these or otherwise\nrandomize the building process to get rid of other sources of measurement bias.\nOn the other hand, in practice, if repeating say compilation is very expensive, and\nwe know that it has relatively little impact on performance or we ﬁnd it out during\nthe following process, we may choose not to repeat it. The threshold we then use for\ncomparisons, however, needs to be set accordingly.\nAfter deciding on the highest level, we continue by identifying lower levels. We need\nto add an additional lower level to the experiment if the variability (say in executions)\nis expected or known to be higher than in the level above (say compilations), while the\ncost of repeating at the lower level is smaller than at the higher level. If we create a\nlevel where it is in fact not needed, we may detect this later in the following process.\nAlso, we can measure the costs of repetition in the following process as a side-effect\n(for example, such cost is the time to build a new binary). In the formulae below, the\nnumber of levels is n+1 (where n is the number of ‘ways’ in the statistical model, which\nis one less than the number of levels in the experiment).\nNumbers of repetitions. Having decided on initial levels of the experiment, we run\nthe experiment a few times to estimate variability at different levels. With our method,\nwe always use the same repetition counts at a particular level (thus all executions have\nthe same number of measurements, all binaries same number of executions, etc.). We\ndenote the repetition counts as ni, where 1 ≤i ≤n + 1 is the level of the experiment\n(nn+1 is the number at highest level, say compilation).\nFor the initial experiment, we can set all these counts to the same value, say 30.\nNote that these counts do not include warming-up various components of the experi-\nment. Usually, we want to evaluate only measurements from a steady-state of every\nexecution. Say that we do so by discarding the ﬁrst c1 measurements of each execution\nbefore collecting the n1 we need. c1 must be found by the experimenter. Repetitions at\nhigher levels, ci, 1 < i ≤n, also may have a non-zero cost. For instance, compilation\nhas notable cost as it takes time to compile. All costs are represented as counts of mea-\nsurements — the cost of compilation is the compilation time divided by the (average)\ntime for a measurement. Precision here is not crucial, as the costs are only needed for\noptimisation.\n\n\n=== PDF page 15 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n15\nTo run the initial experiment, we need to have c1 already and we use ni = 30. During\nthe experiment we collect measurements and also measure the costs for repetition ci\nfor 1 < i ≤n. Based on the costs and the measurements, we decide on optimum counts\nof repetitions at all but the highest level (the derivation is provided in Sections 7.4\nand 7.5) :\nn1 =\n& s\nc1\nT 2\n1\nT 2\n2\n'\n,\n∀i, 1 < i ≤n ni =\n& s\nci\nci−1\nT 2\ni\nT 2\ni+1\n'\n.\nWe obtain Ti, the unbiased estimator of the variance at level i, through an iterative\nprocess. First, we calculate Si, the biased estimator of the variance at level i (formulae\nbelow). Such a wide interval would not be useful in practice, but this\nexample has used only very small repetition counts.\nAlternatively, we could calculate the conﬁdence interval for the ratio of means of the\ntwo systems using the bootstrap method. The bootstrap replicates would be created\nfor both systems in the same way as we demonstrated it for the old system. We would\nthen calculate the ratio of means of these replicates (the ﬁrst of the new system over\nthe ﬁrst of the old system, the second of the new over the second of the old, etc.). From\nthese ratios, we would select the respective quantiles.\n7. FOR SCIENTISTS: THE NEW METHOD AND THE STATISTICS BEHIND IT\nIn this section, we formulate the method in statistical terms, give its assumptions\nand provide proofs. We also discuss alternatives. In the description of the statis-\ntics behind our method, we assume the execution time of the operation of interest\nis a continuous random variable, Y . The range of Y is a subrange of real numbers:\n∃BCET, WCET ∈R : P(Y < BCET) = P(Y > WCET) = 0, where BCET is the best-\ncase execution time and WCET is the worst-case execution time. We assume that the\nexpectation of Y exists and that its variance is ﬁnite, E(Y ) = µ and var(Y ) = σ2. The\n‘operation of interest’ can be anything that is the goal of the measurement, small or\nlarge, depending on the benchmark we use. Y models only the steady state duration of\nthe operation, and we only focus on steady state performance here.\n7.1. Statistical Model for a Hierarchy of Random Effects\n7.1.1. Two-way Classiﬁcation. We ﬁrst describe the model with three levels3 of hierar-\nchy. For clarity of exposition, we shall use our running example of random effects in\ncompilation, execution, and measurement. Later we show a general description for an\n3In our text, the term ‘level’ always refers to levels in a benchmark experiment — it should not be confused\nwith its statistical meaning in texts that address ﬁxed effects models. We only have random effects.\n\n\n=== PDF page 20 ===\n20\nT. Kalibera, R.E. Jones\narbitrary number of levels. The intuition behind the model is simple: the times mea-\nsured in a single execution are randomly distributed, with a mean that is also a ran-\ndom variable (this notion is later formalised using conditional expectation). In turn,\nthe mean of these execution means in a binary4 is a random variable. And, ﬁnally,\nthe mean of these binary means is an unknown constant, the grand mean µ for the\nwhole system we are interested in. We start with a formalisation of the model and its\nproperties.\nWe assume that measurements from a single execution are independent identically\ndistributed. Their mean differs for different executions, as the measurements are in-\nﬂuenced by random effects related to a particular execution. This can be expressed\nusing conditional expectation as E(Y |[µE = m]) = m, where µE is a random vari-\nable which gives a mean m (a number), a mean of measurements of one particu-\nlar execution. The expression says that the random variable E(Y |µE) has the value\nof m when random variable µE has the value of m. We use a shorter notation for\nthis, E(Y |µE) = µE. We assume that the variance of measurements within execution\nis a constant σ2\nE = var(Y |µE) (homoscedastic). This says that the random variable\nvar(Y |µE) has the value of σ2\nE no matter what is the value of µE. We then assume\nthat the µE are independent identically distributed within a given binary, but the\nmean of this distribution is again a random variable within a system: E(µE|µB) = µB.\nWe assume that the variances of execution means within a binary are constant:\nvar(µE|µB) = σ2\nB. The binary mean µB is a random variable for a given system,\nE(µB) = µS, where µS is a constant. We denote the variance of µB as var(µB) = σ2\nS,\nwhere σ2\nS is a constant.\nIt can be shown that E(Y ) = µS (= µ) and that var(Y ) = σ2\nE + σ2\nB + σ2\nS (= σ2).\nWe will do so later for the general case of n-way classiﬁcation. In summary, we have\nthe following random effects model in two-way classiﬁcation (the distribution of the\nobserved execution time is randomly inﬂuenced in two ways, through execution and\ncompilation):\nE(Y ) = µ,\nvar(Y ) = σ2 = σ2\nE + σ2\nB + σ2\nS\nE(Y |µE) = µE,\nvar(Y |µE) = σ2\nE\nE(µE|µB) = µB,\nvar(µE|µB) = σ2\nB\nE(µB) = µ,\nvar(µB) = σ2\nS\n7.1.2. N-way Classiﬁcation. In n-way classiﬁcation, the measurements within an execu-\ntion (Y |µ1) are independent identically distributed with mean µ1 and variance σ2\n1. µ1\nis a random variable. σ2\n1 is a constant:\nE (Y |µ1) = µ1,\nvar (Y |µ1) = σ2\n1.\n(1)\nWhen n, the number of ways of the classiﬁcation, is two or more, the mean of µ1|µ2 is\nµ2, again a random variable. In general,\n∀i, 1 ≤i ≤n −1,\nE (µi|µi+1) = µi+1,\nvar (µi|µi+1) = σ2\ni+1.\n(2)\nFinally, µn|µn+1 is a random variable with mean µn+1, which is a constant:\nE (µn) = µn+1,\nvar (µn) = σ2\nn+1.\n(3)\nLEMMA 7.1 (RULE OF ITERATED EXPECTATIONS).\nIf X and Y are random vari-\nables and the expectations exist, E [E(Y |X)] = E(Y ). Wasserman [2004], Theorem 3.24,\np. 55.\n4We use the term ‘binary’ to denote a single binary executable, that is, a product of compilation. For the\nstatistical model, it is just a factor.\n\n\n=== PDF page 21 ===\nQuantifying Performance Changes with Effect Size Conﬁdence Intervals\n21\nWe will now show that µn+1 = E(Y ) (note that E(Y ) = µ by deﬁnition):\nµ = E(Y ) =(L7.1) E [E (Y |µ1)] =(1) E (µ1)\n∀i, 1 ≤i ≤n −1,\nE (µi) =(L7.1) E [E (µi|µi+1)] =(2) E (µi+1)\nE (µn)\n=(3)\nµn+1\nLEMMA 7.2 (PROPERTY OF CONDITIONAL VARIANCE).\nFor random variables X\nand Y , var(Y ) = E [var(Y |X)] + var [E(Y |X)]. For a 95% interval, we select α = 0.05.\nOut of the wide variety of bootstrap methods, the key decisions here were how to do\nresampling, and how to construct the conﬁdence interval given the simulated means.\nFor resampling, depending on the underlying distribution and the statistic of interest,\nit is sometimes better to resample with replacement only at higher levels of the hier-\narchy, but keep the lower levels intact [Ren et al. 2010; Davison and Hinkley 1997].\nA na¨ıve alternative is then also to ignore the structure of the model and resample at\nrandom from all measurements. We cover these alternatives in our evaluation later.\nThe method of resampling shown in Figure 1, replacement at all levels, seems to work\nbest (or at least not worse than others), and is a common default.\nFor the construction of the conﬁdence interval given the simulated means, we use\nthe percentile method. This method is sensitive to non-symmetrical distributions of the\nstatistic, but it should not be much of a problem here as we have shown earlier that\nY is asymptotically normal. Still, one can easily plug in alternative bootstrap methods\nfor conﬁdence interval construction [Davison and Hinkley 1997]. Statistical software\npackages, such as R, implement plenty of different methods.\n7.2.2. Asymptotic Conﬁdence Interval for One System. A common alternative is based on the delta method.\n7.4. Experiment Planning\nTo obtain an unbiased result and realistic error bars (conﬁdence interval), one has to\nmake sure that all the randomness of the system is encapsulated by the experiment.\nFor example, if the system of interest has non-deterministic compilation and different\nbinaries also differ in performance, one has to run multiple binaries within that exper-\niment. The cost of building a new binary can be high, e.g. for the Mono platform, which\nwe use for validation of our method, the compilation time was about 20 minutes. If\nthe ﬂuctuations in performance due to non-determinism in compilation are small (σS\nis small) compared to, say, the one due to non-determinism in execution (σB), which\nis the usual case in our experiments, time allocated to experimentation would be bet-\nter spent by running the existing binaries multiple times rather than building new\nbinaries. We can formalise such trade-offs and, based on initial experiments that re-\npeat a few times at all levels to estimate the variances, we can calculate the optimum\nnumber of repetitions at each level that results in the most precise result for a given\nexperimentation time.\nLet us assume the random effects model in n-way classiﬁcation of Section 7.1. We\nhave run initial experiments to obtain estimates of the variances σ2\n1, σ2\n2, . . . , σ2\nn+1. We\nwill discuss later which estimators can be used. We also assume we know the cost of a\nnew repetition at the (non-top) levels, c1, c2, . . . , cn, so that the total cost of experimen-\ntation is\nc = (cn + . . . (c3 + (c2 + (c1 + n1)n2)n3 . . .)nn)nn+1\n(13)\nThe unit of the costs (c, ci) is the number of (the lowest-level) measurements that could\nbe obtained in that time. The practical meaning of the costs and their use have been\ndescribed informally in Section 6.1. With three levels only, the total cost would be\nc = (c2 + (c1 + n1)n2)n3\nwhere n1 is the number of steady state measurements, c1 is the number of warm-up\nmeasurements of an execution that are not included into summarization (cost of a new\nexecution), n2 is the number of executions, c2 is the number of measurements that\ncould be done in the time needed to build one binary, and n3 is the number of binaries.\nThe costs ci can be calculated during the initial experiments that have to be executed\nto estimate the variances σ2\ni . For the purpose of experiment planning, precise estimates\nof the costs are not needed — the planning is a back-of-the-envelope calculation. For\ninstance, we can use averages from the initial experiments.
